New solutions of multidimensional fuzzy fractional coupled Burgers systems via He Laplace Carson algorithm
Authors: Mubashir Qayyum, Sidra Nayab, Omar Khan, Jihad Younis, Abdou Al Zubaidi, Mazhar Hussain, Syed Tauseef Saeed
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
The coupled Burgers model, a system of nonlinear partial differential equations, is widely used to describe complex interactions in various fields, including fluid dynamics, traffic flow, and biological systems. It provides insights into phenomena such as shock waves, turbulence, pattern formation, and transitions from order to chaos. This study applies the He-Laplace-Carson (HLC) scheme to solve the (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers model under fuzzy-fractional conditions. The HLC scheme, which combines the homotopy perturbation method with the Laplace-Carson transform, is utilized to obtain accurate numerical solutions for these highly nonlinear systems. The model is analyzed in both fractional and fuzzy-fractional frameworks, enabling a comprehensive examination across lower and upper bounds, as well as the traditional crisp case. Two benchmark problems are investigated to evaluate the influence of Caputo fractional derivatives, with residual error analysis validating the accuracy of the results. Detailed two and three-dimensional visualizations, along with innovative contour plots, provide deeper physical interpretations of the model’s behavior. The findings demonstrate the robustness and effectiveness of the proposed methodology in addressing complex mathematical models that arise in diverse scientific applications.

Introduction

Fractional calculus1 is a generalized toolkit which offers a flexible framework for modeling real phenomena. Fractional differential equations find widespread application in various fields, including mathematical biology2, engineering3, chemistry4, and physics5. Notably, they have been instrumental in modeling viscoelasticity6, diffusion7, heat conduction8, dynamical systems9, and numerous other significant areas10–12

Due to its widespread importance, numerous methodologies have been developed to recover solutions of fractional order equations13. In last few decades, different concepts of fractional derivatives have emerged, including Riemann-Liouville14, Caputo15, Caputo-Fabrizio16, Riesz17, and Atangana-Baleanu18. To address these challenges, a number of numerical and semi-analytical methodologies are proposed, such as residual power series19, homotopy analysis method20, differential transform method21, variation iteration method22, and family of homotopy perturbation method23,24, and Adam-Bashforth-Moulton25.

Fractional calculus has gained significant attention for its ability to accurately model memory and hereditary properties in complex systems, which are often difficult to capture using classical integer-order models. Its mathematical flexibility allows for a more precise description of processes exhibiting anomalous diffusion, nonlocal behavior, and long-term dependencies. Recent studies have demonstrated its wide-ranging applicability in various disciplines, including physical sciences, biomedical modeling, and engineering problems such as heat transfer, viscoelasticity, fluid dynamics, and signal processing26–28. These developments have established fractional calculus as a powerful framework for improving the accuracy and robustness of complex system modeling. These works further highlight the advantages of fractional-order models over classical integer-order formulations, particularly in capturing anomalous diffusion and non-local dynamics. Several definitions of fractional derivatives exist, including the Riemann-Liouville, Grünwald-Letnikov, and Caputo formulations. Among these, the Caputo derivative is particularly preferred for initial value problems because it allows the use of standard (integer-order) initial conditions, which are easier to interpret physically and experimentally. Furthermore, Caputo’s formulation yields solutions that are continuous at the origin, making it more suitable for the fractional analysis of real-world systems29,30.

The evolution of the He-Laplace-Carson algorithm can be described as the amalgamation of the homotopy perturbation31 with one of integral transform namely Laplace-Carson transform32 in order to provide much more accurate numerical solution of high dimensional non-linear systems of partial fractional differential equations where closed form solutions are either impossible or inaccurate to acquire by the traditional methods widely used in literature. Different studies using this method include fuzzy-fractional modeling and simulation of electric circuits using extended He-Laplace-Carson algorithm33, analysis of virus spread in WSN34, and Laplace-Carson integral transform for fractional order initial value problems35.

The Burgers’ equation was initially proposed by Harry Bateman as a one-dimensional model36 in 1915, which was further investigated in37. Since then, extensive research has been conducted on the Burgers’ equation, demonstrating its effectiveness in modeling phenomena such as shock waves38, turbulence39, heat conduction40, acoustic waves41, and others. Extensive research has been conducted on different variants of Burgers’ equation, both in integer and non-integer order frameworks, employing a wide range of techniques to derive solutions. Some prominent advancements in the world of Burgers’s equations were created by Biler et al. in creating the concept of fractal Burgers’ equations42, further Fletcher et al. analyzed the one-and two-dimensional Burgers’ equations numerically using FDM and FEM. Esipov et al. presented concept of coupled Burgers equations as a model of polydispersive sedimentation43, Wazwaz gave multiple-front solutions for the Burgers equation and the coupled Burgers equations44. Zhao et al. presented the new numerical method for solving the system of two-dimensional Burgers’ equations45, furthering that work Zou et al. provided solutions for both two and three dimensional Burgers’ equations46.

Lump et al. built on the previous work and put forth a method for solving (2 + 1) dimensional Burgers’s equations. Zu et al. dealt with (3 + 1) dimensional Burgers equations in case of water waves47. Lian et al. studied (3 + 1)-dimensional time-fractional modified Burgers equation for dust ion-acoustic waves49, while Alsaud et al. employed Adomian decomposition method on the equations in49. Iqbal et al.50 presented the fractional study of the non-linear Burgers’ equations via a semi-analytical technique such as rapid convergence McLaurin series. Sultan et al.51 wrote on the flow of generalized Burgers fluid between parallel walls induced by rectified sine pulses stress. Also, Qayumm et al.52 wrote on innovative solutions of Fuzzy-Fractional Fisher Models via Optimal He-Laplace Algorithm. Abbas et al.53 discussed the analysis of fractionalized Brinkman flow in the presence of diffusion effect. Hence, it can be observed that there has been quite some work done on the traditional non-coupled Burgers’ model and there has also been some contribution in the fuzzy number theory for other models, however there hasn’t been any work done when it comes to coupled Burgers’ model in a fuzzy fractional limelight which is the key novelty of our work done.

The main focus of this study as described in the manuscript is to firstly study the (3 + 1) dimensional time-fractional Burgers’ model to find accurate numerical or approximate solutions of the model under consideration, which is justified through numerical validation in the form of comprehensive residual errors computation. Secondly, the aura of uncertainty which is engraved in the study of the (3 + 1) dimensional fractional coupled Burgers’ model has been tackled by the inclusion of fuzziness into the model itself to offer a more detailed understanding of the Burgers’ equations at lower and upper bounds rather than only considering the central framework. Shock waves, various kinds of fluid flows, turbulence, population wave models and viscoelasticity etc. and all other applications of Burgers’ models are themselves uncertain phenomenons and therefore fuzziness into the system is more capable of capturing the model under consideration. Furthermore, to justify the importance of our novel idea, we have also added detailed tabular error analyzes as well as created 2D, 3D and contour plots for both the fractional and fuzzy-fractional environments.

This work indicatively integrates fuzzy theory within a fractional calculus framework to analyze the (3 + 1)D fuzzy-fractional Burgers’ model, enabling the interpretation of its behavior through upper and lower bounds, a departure from traditional deterministic approaches. Prior studies on the Burgers’ model have largely focused on integer-order formulations or, when considering fractional derivatives, have overlooked the incorporation of fuzzy numbers, a significant gap given the inherent uncertainties in real-world phenomena. By introducing fuzziness to account for imprecise parameters, this research significantly expands the analytical scope, allowing for a more nuanced and realistic understanding of the system’s dynamics under uncertainty, a capability absent in conventional treatments and opening an entirely new domain of study for the Burgers’ model. For future research prospects, a specific application of real-life phenomenon involving the use of coupled Burgers’ model can be investigated using the proposed methodology. Our proposed method offers analytical approximations for the coupled (3 + 1) Burgers’ model without discretization, handling nonlinearity well, but faces challenges with guaranteed convergence, complexity in higher dimensions, the inverse Laplace transform, and dependence on initial choices, ultimately providing approximate solutions needing validation therefore, we have presented detailed numerical validations in form of residual errors so there remains no shred of doubt in the convergence and accuracy of solutions obtained through our method.

In rest of the paper, Preliminaries are presented in Section “Preliminaries”, the convergence and error estimation is provided in Section “Convergence and error estimation”, followed by the formulation Section “Formulation of fractional and fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model”. Further, the general methodology for He-Laplace-Carson procedure is given in Section “He-Laplace-Carson algorithm for fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1)-dimensional PDEs” and the application of methodology is explained in Section “Application of He-Laplace Carson algorithm to fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model”. Furthermore, the results and discussion is provided in Section “Results and discussion” and finally the conclusion is drawn in Section “Conclusion”.

Preliminaries

Definition 1

The Laplace-Carson transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}$$\end{document}35 of the fuzzy function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} is defined as:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {C}\{\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)\} = \tilde{\mathfrak {Y}}(\mathbbm {p}) = \mathbbm {p} \int _0^{\infty } \tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z) e^{-\mathbbm {p}t} \, dt, \quad \mathbbm {p} \in \mathbb {R}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Definition 2

The inverse Laplace-Carson transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}^{-1}$$\end{document}54 of the fuzzy function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\mathfrak {Y}}(\mathbbm {p})$$\end{document} can be formulated as:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {C}^{-1}\{\tilde{\mathfrak {Y}}(\mathbbm {p})\} = {\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z) = \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int _{\xi - i\infty }^{\xi + i\infty } \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}} \mathfrak {\tilde{Y}}(\mathbbm {p}) e^{\mathbbm {p}t} \, d\mathbbm {p}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi$$\end{document} is a real number.

Definition 3

The Caputo’s time-fractional derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{C}_{}{\mathcal {\mathfrak {D}}}_t^\beta$$\end{document}55, for any fuzzy function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} can be defined as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ^{C}_{}{\mathcal {D}}_t^\beta \{{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)\} = \frac{1}{\Gamma (\nu -\beta )} \int _{0}^{t} (t-\psi )^{\nu -\beta -1}{\tilde{\chi }}^{(\nu )}(\psi ,x,y,z) d\psi , \hspace{1cm} \nu -1 < \beta \le \nu , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi$$\end{document} is any random dummy variable.

Definition 4

The Laplace-Carson transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {C}$$\end{document}56 applied to the Caputo fractional derivative (3) leads to:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathfrak {C}\left\{ ^{C}_{}{\mathcal {D}}_t^\beta {\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z) \right\} = \mathbbm {p}^\beta \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - \sum _{l=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^{\beta - l} {\tilde{\chi }}^{(l)}(x,y,z,0),\hspace{1cm} \nu -1 < \beta \le \nu . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Definition 7

For three numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathfrak {t}_1, \mathfrak {t}_2, \mathfrak {t}_3)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {t}_1< \mathfrak {t}_2 < \mathfrak {t}_3$$\end{document}, a fuzzy number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{S}$$\end{document} is called a triangular fuzzy number (TFN)57 if it forms a triangle. The membership function of a TFN is given by:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \epsilon (\textrm{u}, \mathfrak {t}_1, \mathfrak {t}_2,\mathfrak {t}_3) = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 0, & \textrm{u} \le \mathfrak {t}_1, \\ \frac{\textrm{u} - \mathfrak {t}_1}{\mathfrak {t}_2- \mathfrak {t}_1}, & \mathfrak {t}_1 \le \textrm{u}\le \mathfrak {t}_2, \\ \frac{t_3 - \textrm{u}}{t_3 - \mathfrak {t}_2}, & \mathfrak {t}_2 \le \textrm{u} \le \mathfrak {t}_3, \\ 0, & \textrm{u} \ge \mathfrak {t}_3. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The interval form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\textrm{S}}$$\end{document} can be expressed as:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\textrm{S}} = [\underline{\textrm{u}}, \overline{\textrm{u}}] = [\mathfrak {t}_1 + (\mathfrak {t}_2 - \mathfrak {t}_1)p, \quad \mathfrak {t}_3 - (\mathfrak {t}_3 - \mathfrak {t}_2){p}], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\underline{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document} represents the lower bound and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\textrm{u}}$$\end{document} depicts the upper bound for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [0, 1]$$\end{document}. Here, r is also termed as the p-cut.

Convergence and error estimation

Theorem 1

For any two functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\chi }}_m(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} defined within a Banach space, the approximate solution of the fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1)-dimensional model approaches its exact solution, with the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {B}$$\end{document} is confined to the interval \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,1)$$\end{document}.

Proof

Consider the sequence of partial sums \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathcal {S}_m\}$$\end{document}. We need to demonstrate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} forms a Cauchy sequence in the Banach space. For this purpose, let us examine:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {S}_{m+1}(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) \Vert&= \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_{m+1}(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\le \mathcal {B} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_m(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\le \mathcal {B}^2 \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_{m-1}(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\hspace{0.2cm}\vdots \\&\le \mathcal {B}^{m+1} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where for the partial sums \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m \ge n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m, n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}, applying the triangle inequality results in:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z) \Vert&= \Vert (\mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_{m-1}(t,x,y,z)) + (\mathcal {S}_{m-1}(t,x,y,z) \\&\quad - \mathcal {S}_{m-2}(t,x,y,z)) + \cdots + (\mathcal {S}_{n+1}(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_{n}(t,x,y,z)) \Vert \\&\le \Vert \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_{m-1}(t,x,y,z) \Vert + \Vert \mathcal {S}_{m-1}(t,x,y,z) \\&\quad - \mathcal {S}_{m-2}(t,x,y,z) \Vert + \cdots + \Vert \mathcal {S}_{n+1}(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_{n}(t,x,y,z) \Vert . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}After substituting (8) in (9) we get:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z) \Vert&\le \mathcal {B}^{m} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert + \mathcal {B}^{m-1} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert \Vert + \cdots \\&\quad + \mathcal {B}^{m+1} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\le (\mathcal {B}^{m} + \mathcal {B}^{m-1} + \cdots + \mathcal {B}^{m+1}) \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\le \mathcal {B}^{m+1}(\mathcal {B}^{m-n-1} + \mathcal {B}^{m-n-2} + \cdots +\mathcal {B} + 1) \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert \\&\le \mathcal {B}^{m+1} \left( \frac{1 - \mathcal {B}^{m-n}}{1 - \mathcal {B}} \right) \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}As we know that, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \mathcal {B} < 1$$\end{document}, thus it gives \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1- \mathcal {B}^{m-n} < 1$$\end{document}. Consequently,11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z) \Vert&\le \frac{\mathcal {B}^{m+1}}{{1 - \mathcal {B}}}\hspace{0.1cm}{\max |{\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z)|}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Since, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\chi }}_0$$\end{document} is already bounded therefore,12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{m,n \rightarrow \infty } \Vert \mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z) - \mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z) \Vert = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Equation (12) demonstrates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}_m(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} is a Cauchy sequence within a Banach space.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Theorem 2

The solution for any general fuzzy-fractional differential model obtained through the He-Laplace-Carson algorithm is associated with a maximum absolute truncation error represented as:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigg |{\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)} - \sum _{k=0}^{m} {\tilde{\chi }}_k(t,x,y,z) \Bigg | \le \frac{B_{m+1}}{1 - B} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Proof

Extracting the relation (10) and considering we get:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)} - \mathcal {S}_n(t,x,y,z) \Vert \le \mathcal {B}^{m+1} \left( \frac{1 - \mathcal {B}^{m-n}}{1 - \mathcal {B}} \right) \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}As we know that, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \mathcal {B} < 1$$\end{document}, this implies that: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1- \mathcal {B}^{m-n} < 1$$\end{document}. Therefore, we have:15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Bigg | {\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)} - \sum _{k=0}^{m} {\tilde{\chi }}_k(t,x,y,z) \Bigg | \le \frac{B_{m+1}}{1 - B} \Vert {\tilde{\chi }}_0(t,x,y,z) \Vert , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}which proves the required notion. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square$$\end{document}

Formulation of fractional and fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model

In the current study, we examine the following system49 to demonstrate the proposed methodology.16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&D_t^\beta \Psi + \Psi \Psi _x + \varphi \Psi _y + \chi \Psi _z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \Psi _{xx} + \Psi _{yy} + \Psi _{zz} \right) + \Psi , \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D_t^\beta \varphi + \Psi \varphi _x + \varphi \varphi _y + \chi \varphi _z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \varphi _{xx} + \varphi _{yy} + \varphi _{zz} \right) + \varphi , \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D_t^\beta \chi + \Psi \chi _x + \varphi \chi _y + \chi \chi _z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \chi _{xx} + \chi _{yy} + \chi _{zz} \right) + \chi , \quad x, y, z, t > 0, \\&\nu -1< \beta < \nu . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with the following initial conditions:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Psi (x, y, z, 0)&= f_1(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \varphi (x, y, z, 0)&= g_1(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \chi (x, y, z, 0)&= h_1(x, y, z). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_t^\beta = \frac{\partial ^\beta }{\partial t^\beta }$$\end{document} represents the fractional Caputo derivative. The functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document} denote the velocity components to be determined, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1$$\end{document} are given functions. Additionally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {Re}$$\end{document} refers to the Reynolds number.

Consider also a slightly different form of the Burgers’s equation49 which considers singular (3 + 1)-dimensional fractional form. To examine the idea, we consider a general form of the singular (3 + 1)-dimensional time-fractional coupled Burgers’ equation given by:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} D_t^\beta \Psi&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \Psi _x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \Psi _y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \Psi _z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \Psi \Psi _x - \frac{1}{y} \varphi \Psi _y - \frac{1}{z} \chi \Psi _z + \Psi , \\ D_t^\beta \varphi&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \varphi _x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \varphi _y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \varphi _z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \Psi \varphi _x - \frac{1}{y} \varphi \varphi _y - \frac{1}{z} \chi \varphi _z + \varphi , \\ D_t^\beta \chi&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \chi _x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \chi _y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \chi _z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \Psi \chi _x - \frac{1}{y} \varphi \chi _y - \frac{1}{z} \chi \chi _z + \chi , \\ \nu -1&< \beta < \nu . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with the initial conditions as follows:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Psi (x, y, z, 0)&= f_2(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \varphi (x, y, z, 0)&= g_2(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \chi (x, y, z, 0)&= h_2(x, y, z). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_t^\beta = \frac{\partial ^\beta }{\partial t^\beta }$$\end{document} is the fractional Caputo derivative. The terms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{x} (x \Psi _x)_x$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{y} (y \Psi _y)_y$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{z} (z \chi _z)_z$$\end{document} are referred to as Bessel operators. Additionally, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (x, y, z, t)$$\end{document} represent the velocity components, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_2(x, y, z)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_2(x, y, z)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_2(x, y, z)$$\end{document} are known functions.

As outlined above, the next part will focus on introducing uncertainty into the system conditions. The newly introduced fuzzy parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Q}_i$$\end{document} where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1(1)3$$\end{document} satisfy the definition of fuzzy numbers given in section 2. Consequently, (16) along with the initial conditions (17), the new fuzzy fractional system of (3 + 1)-dimensional coupled Burgers’ equations can be expressed as:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&D_t^\beta \tilde{\Psi } + \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\Psi }_x + \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\Psi }_y + \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\Psi }_z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \tilde{\Psi }_{xx} + \tilde{\Psi }_{yy} + \tilde{\Psi }_{zz} \right) + \tilde{\Psi }, \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D_t^\beta \tilde{\varphi } + \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\varphi }_x + \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\varphi }_y + \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\varphi }_z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \tilde{\varphi }_{xx} + \tilde{\varphi }_{yy} + \tilde{\varphi }_{zz} \right) + \tilde{\varphi }, \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D_t^\beta \tilde{\chi } + \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\chi }_x + \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\chi }_y + \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\chi }_z = \frac{1}{\text {Re}} \left( \tilde{\chi }_{xx} + \tilde{\chi }_{yy} + \tilde{\chi }_{zz} \right) + \tilde{\chi }, \quad x, y, z, t > 0, \\&\nu -1< \beta < \nu . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with the following initial conditions:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{\Psi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_1}\hspace{0.1cm} f_1(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \tilde{\varphi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_2}\hspace{0.1cm} g_1(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \tilde{\chi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_3}\hspace{0.1cm} h_1(x, y, z). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In a similar way, singular (3 + 1)-dimensional time-fractional coupled Burgers’ system in (18) and (19) becomes:22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} D_t^\beta \tilde{\Psi }&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\Psi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\Psi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\Psi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\Psi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\Psi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\Psi }_z + \tilde{\Psi }, \\ D_t^\beta \tilde{\varphi }&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\varphi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\varphi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\varphi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\varphi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\varphi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\varphi }_z + \tilde{\varphi }, \\ D_t^\beta \tilde{\chi }&= \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\chi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\chi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\chi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\chi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\chi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\chi }_z + \tilde{\chi }, \\ \nu -1&< \beta < \nu . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with the initial conditions as follows:23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{\Psi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_1}\hspace{0.1cm} f_2(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \tilde{\varphi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_2} \hspace{0.1cm} g_2(x, y, z), \hspace{0.5cm} \tilde{\chi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q_3} \hspace{0.1cm}h_2(x, y, z). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In the next section we will provide proper framework to explain how the proposed method i.e. He-Laplace-Carson algorithm can be applied effectively to any general system of (3 + 1)-dimensional equations in order to provide with a clear outline of the efficacy and applicability of our method to such complex highly non-linear systems of partial differential equations.

He-Laplace-Carson algorithm for fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1)-dimensional PDEs

Many complex scenarios, like the case of (3 + 1) coupled Burgers’ model, defy exact mathematical solutions. To address this, we utilized the He-Laplace-Carson algorithm. This innovative approach enables us to obtain numerical and series solutions for such intricate complicated models. In this section, we outline a general methodology for applying the He-Laplace-Carson algorithm to solve systems of fuzzy-fractional partial differential equations. Let we consider the following system of fuzzy-fractional partial differential equations:24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [\tilde{\Psi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {L} [\tilde{\Psi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {N}[\tilde{\Psi }(t,x,y,z)] - r_{1}(t,x,y,z) = 0, \\&^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [\tilde{\varphi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {L} [\tilde{\varphi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {N}[\tilde{\varphi }(t,x,y,z)] - r_{2}(t,x,y,z) = 0, \\&^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {L} [\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)] + \mathcal {N}[\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)] - r_{3}(t,x,y,z) = 0, \\&\nu -1 < \beta \le \nu , \hspace{0.2cm} t >0, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}having initial conditions:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{\Psi }(t,x,y,z) = \zeta _{1}, \ \ \tilde{\varphi }(t,x,y,z) = \zeta _{2}, \ \ \tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z) = \zeta _{3}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Psi }(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\varphi }(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\chi }(t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} are fuzzy functions. By application of definition 6 and 7, we arrive at:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{\Psi }(x,y,z,t; p)&= \left[ {\underline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p), {\overline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\right] , \\ \tilde{\varphi }(x,y,z,t; p)&= \left[ {\underline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p), {\overline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\right] , \\ \tilde{\chi }(x,y,z,t; p)&= \left[ {\underline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p), {\overline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\right] , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} are the lower bound, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} are the upper bounds of solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Psi }(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\varphi }(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\chi }(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} respectively. By making use of definitions from (26) into (24), we obtain the modified form:27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\left[ ^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\underline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\overline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\underline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {L}[{\overline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] \right. \\ &\quad +\left[ \mathcal {N}[{\underline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {N}[ {\overline{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{1}(t) = 0,\\&\left[ ^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\underline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\overline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\underline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {L}[{\overline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] \right. \\ &\quad +\left[ \mathcal {N}[{\underline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {N}[ {\overline{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{2}(t) = 0,\\&\left[ ^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\underline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\overline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\underline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {L}[{\overline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] \right. \\ &\quad +\left[ \mathcal {N}[{\underline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)],\mathcal {N}[ {\overline{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{3}(t) = 0.\\ \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 1:

Apply Laplace Carson transform to (24) as28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathfrak {C}[^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathfrak {C} \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{1}(t) = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}[^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathfrak {C} \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{2}(t) = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}[^{C}_{}{\mathfrak {D}}_t^\beta [{\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathfrak {C} \left[ \mathcal {L}[ {\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \right] - r_{3}(t) = 0.\\ \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 2:

The fractional differential property of Laplace-Carson transform on above system of equations leads to:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\Psi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \\ &\quad + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)]\} = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\varphi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \\ &\quad + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)]\} = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\chi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)] \\ &\quad + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)]\} = 0. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 3:

Next, construct homotopies for (24), we get:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathfrak {H}_1: (1 - \textrm{d}) \left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\Psi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - {\tilde{\Psi }}_0 \right) \\&\quad + \textrm{d}\left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\Psi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\Psi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) \right. \\ &\quad \left. + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\Psi }}(t,x,y,z)]+ \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\Psi }}(t,x,y,z)]\}\right) =0,\\&\mathfrak {H}_2: (1 - \textrm{d}) \left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\varphi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - {\tilde{\varphi }}_0 \right) \\&\quad +\textrm{d}\left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\varphi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\varphi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) \right. \\ &\quad + \left. \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}{[}{\tilde{\varphi }}(t,x,y,z)]+ \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\varphi }}(t,x,y,z)]\}\right) =0,\\&\mathfrak {H}_3: (1 - \textrm{d}) \left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - {\tilde{\chi }}_0 \right) \\&\quad + \textrm{d}\left( \mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)\} - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^ {\beta - k} {{\tilde{\chi }}}^{(k)}(x,y,z,0) \right. \\ &\quad + \left. \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)]+ \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)]\}\right) =0. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 4:

Power series expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)$$\end{document} are:31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\tilde{\Psi }}(x,y,z,t; p)&= \sum _{z=0}^{\infty } \textrm{d}^z {\tilde{\Psi }}_z(x,y,z,t; p),\\ {\tilde{\varphi }}(x,y,z,t; p)&= \sum _{z=0}^{\infty } \textrm{d}^z {\tilde{\varphi }}_z(x,y,z,t; p),\\ {\tilde{\chi }}(x,y,z,t; p)&= \sum _{z=0}^{\infty } \textrm{d}^z {\tilde{\chi }}_z(x,y,z,t; p). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 5:

After using Equation 31 in Equation 30 gives the following:

At \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}^{1}$$\end{document}:32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\Psi }}_{1}\} + {\tilde{\Psi }}_0 - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^\beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^{\beta -k} {\tilde{\Psi }}^{(k)}(0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\Psi }}_0] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\Psi }}_0]\} = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\varphi }}_{1}\} + {\tilde{\varphi }}_0 - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^\beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^{\beta -k} {\tilde{\varphi }}^{(k)}(0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\varphi }}_0] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\varphi }}_0]\} = 0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}_{1}\} + {\tilde{\chi }}_0 - \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^\beta }\right) \sum _{k=0}^{\nu -1} \mathbbm {p}^{\beta -k} {\tilde{\chi }}^{(k)}(0) + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\chi }}_0] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\chi }}_0]\} = 0. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Generally at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{d}^{k}$$\end{document}, we have:33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\Psi }}_{k}\} + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\Psi }}_{k-1}] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\Psi }}_{k-1}]\} =0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\varphi }}_{k}\} + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\varphi }}_{k-1}] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\varphi }}_{k-1}]\} =0,\\&\mathfrak {C}\{{\tilde{\chi }}_{k}\} + \left( \frac{1}{\mathbbm {p}^ \beta } \right) \mathfrak {C}\{\mathcal {L}[{\tilde{\chi }}_{k-1}] + \mathcal {N}[{\tilde{\chi }}_{k-1}]\} =0. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Operation 6:

By applying inverse of Laplace-Carson transform, we obtained the following series solution as34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} {\tilde{\Psi }}(t,x,y,z)&= {\tilde{\Psi }}_{0}(t,x,y,z) + + {\tilde{\Psi }}_{2}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\Psi }}_{3}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\Psi }}_{4}(t,x,y,z) + \cdots \\ {\tilde{\varphi }}(t,x,y,z)&= {\tilde{\varphi }}_{0}(t,x,y,z) + + {\tilde{\varphi }}_{2}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\varphi }}_{3}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\varphi }}_{4}(t,x,y,z) + \cdots \\ {\tilde{\chi }}(t,x,y,z)&= {\tilde{\chi }}_{0}(t,x,y,z) + + {\tilde{\chi }}_{2}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\chi }}_{3}(t,x,y,z) + {\tilde{\chi }}_{4}(t,x,y,z) + \cdots \\ \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The primary difference between fuzzy and non-fuzzy approaches lies in the parameter ’p-cut’. In non-fuzzy systems, ’p-cut’ is consistently set to 1, effectively reducing the fuzzy solution to its crisp (precise) form. Consequently, the overall solution methodology for non-fuzzy fractional systems largely mirrors that of fuzzy systems, with the sole variation being the assigned ’p-cut’ value. Subsequent sections will provide a more in-depth analysis to further clarify the differences between these two types of fractional systems.

The numerical solutions were obtained using self-developed Mathematica codes based on the proposed He Laplace-Carson (HLC) algorithm. Parametric values, including the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} and other spatial parameters, were chosen within suitable ranges to test the method’s accuracy and stability, as no experimental data were available. Application of He-Laplace Carson algorithm to fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model

Test problem 1

Consider our first problem of fuzzy fractional system of differential equations for the Burgers’ model same as the one in (20) with initial conditions 21 in the form of a numerical question as follows:35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&D^\beta _t \tilde{\Psi }+ \tilde{\Psi }\tilde{\Psi }_x + \tilde{\varphi }\tilde{\Psi }_y + \tilde{\chi }\tilde{\Psi }_z = \tilde{\Psi }_{xx} + \tilde{\Psi }_{yy} + \tilde{\Psi }_{zz} + \tilde{\Psi }, \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D^\beta _t \tilde{\varphi }+ \Psi \tilde{\varphi }_x + \tilde{\varphi }\tilde{\varphi }_y + \tilde{\chi }\tilde{\varphi }_z = \tilde{\varphi }_{xx} + \tilde{\varphi }_{yy} + \tilde{\varphi }_{zz} + \tilde{\varphi }, \quad x, y, z, t> 0, \\&D^\beta _t \tilde{\chi }+ \tilde{\Psi }\tilde{\chi }_x + \tilde{\varphi }\tilde{\chi }_y + \tilde{\chi }\tilde{\chi }_z = \tilde{\chi }_{xx} + \tilde{\chi }_{yy} + \tilde{\chi }_{zz} + \tilde{\chi }, \quad x, y, z, t > 0, \\&0< \beta < 1. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with initial conditions:36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{\Psi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q}_1(2x - y - z),\ \ \tilde{\varphi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q}_2(2x - y - z), \ \ \tilde{\chi }(x, y, z, 0)&= \tilde{Q}_3(2x - y - z). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Test problem 2

Consider our second problem of fuzzy fractional system for the Burgers’ model same as the one in equation 22 with initial conditions 23 as follows:37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&D^\beta _t \tilde{\Psi } = \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\Psi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\Psi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\Psi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\Psi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\Psi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\Psi }_z + \tilde{\Psi }, \\&D^\beta _t \tilde{\varphi } = \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\varphi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\varphi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\varphi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\varphi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\varphi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\varphi }_z + \tilde{\varphi }, \\&D^\beta _t \tilde{\chi } = \frac{1}{x} \left( x \tilde{\chi }_x \right) _x + \frac{1}{y} \left( y \tilde{\chi }_y \right) _y + \frac{1}{z} \left( z \tilde{\chi }_z \right) _z - \frac{1}{x} \tilde{\Psi } \tilde{\chi }_x - \frac{1}{y} \tilde{\varphi } \tilde{\chi }_y - \frac{1}{z} \tilde{\chi } \tilde{\chi }_z + \tilde{\chi }, \\&x,y,z,t > 0 \quad 0< \beta < 1 \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}with initial conditions:38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\tilde{\Psi }(x, y, z, 0) = \tilde{Q}_1 \left( 2x^2 - y^2 - z^2 \right) , \\&\tilde{\varphi }(x, y, z, 0) = \tilde{Q}_2 \left( 2x^2 - y^2 - z^2 \right) , \\&\tilde{\chi }(x, y, z, 0) = \tilde{Q}_3 \left( 2x^2 - y^2 - z^2 \right) . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The triangular fuzzy numbers in both the problems above i.e. 35 with 36 and 37 and 38 have intervals \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Q}_1 = (0.8, 1, 1.5)$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Q}_2 = (0.8, 1, 1.5)$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{Q}_3 = (0.8, 1, 1.5)$$\end{document}. By utilizing Definition 6 and 7, we can express them in parametric form as follows:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \tilde{Q}_1&= [0.8 + 0.2p, 1.5-0.5p], \\ \tilde{Q}_2&= [0.8 + 0.2p, 1.5-0.5p], \\ \tilde{Q}_3&= [0.8 + 0.2p, 1.5-0.5p]. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}By implementing the proposed solution methodology as discussed in Section “5”, on Test Problem 1 and 2, the solution of fuzzy-fractional Burgers’ systems has been recovers. These solutions are then thoroughly analyzed in tabular and graphical environment, which will be detailed in the subsequent sections.

Results and discussion

In this section firstly, we will analyze the (3 + 1) dimensional fractional Burgers’ model, examining both the error analysis and visual representations of its results. Secondly, we will evaluate the fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional Burgers’ model after incorporating fuzziness into the original model. This second part aims to provide a more comprehensive assessment of the (3 + 1) dimensional Burgers’ model’s impact within the relevant context. We will be presenting two distinct cases of coupled Burgers’s model in form of two different problems, as shown in Eqs. (35) with (36) and (37) with (38) in both fractional and fuzzy-fractional environments individually.Fig. 1Fractional 2D analysis of problem 1.Fig. 2Fractional 3D analysis of problem 1 using Mathematica 12.0.Fig. 3Fractional contour analysis of problem 1 using Mathematica 12.0.

Fractional 2D analysis of problem 1.

Fractional 3D analysis of problem 1 using Mathematica 12.0.

Fractional contour analysis of problem 1 using Mathematica 12.0.

Fractional analysis of problem 1

In order to validate the effectiveness of our method on the (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model, we have provided with detailed tabular results in the form of solutions and their residuals along various fractional orders i.e. from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0.8$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0.99$$\end{document} as can be seen in Tables 1, 2, 3 and 4.

Furthermore, for investigating the behavior of Burgers’ model, we have also provided comprehensive 2D, 3D and contour analysis of problem 1 in a fractional framework. It can be seen in Fig. 1 part (a), with the rise of the x-values with time, the solution profile for the velocity component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} as well as the other two also rises. Whereas with time, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} seems to be decreasing with rising values of y-vlaues and z-values, as displayed in parts (b) and (c). Similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} shows a direct relation with both time t and fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document}.

In case of 3D analysis, we have created solution plots along the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} and time t where by varying time from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=100$$\end{document} gradually, we have shown how the velocity component seems to be changing, as can be seen in Fig. 2. Initially, at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=1$$\end{document} the behavior was different (see part (a)) however, with increasing time i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=5$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=10$$\end{document} and finally at time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t=100$$\end{document}, the trend of increase becomes constant as can be seen in parts (b), (c) and (d) respectively.

The contour analysis performed on the same pattern as the 3D analysis provides with a much more detailed and visually clear picture of the behavior of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document}, as seen in Fig. 3.Fig. 4Fuzzy-fractional 2D analysis of problem 1. Further, Fig. 4 displays the two dimensional analysis of the problem under consideration. It can be seen in parts (a)–(c), the p-cut plotting has been provided at lower bound, the combined form and the upper bounds respectively, confirming use of triangular fuzzy numbers. Parts (d)-(e) on the other hand focuses on showing the direct relation of fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} at both the lower and upper bounds when time is rising.
