Fuzzy-fractional analysis of problem 2

This portion contains the fuzzy-fractional interpretation of the (3 + 1) coupled burgers’ model while considering the solution of problem 2 through our proposed methodology. Firstly, we provided numerical validation at both lower and upper bounds in order to establish the effectiveness and applicability of our method while tackling such highly complex (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ model. For various fractional orders ranging from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0.8$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =0.95$$\end{document}, we have provided the solution as well as residual errors at both bounds to account for the validation of our results, as can be seen in Table 10. Furthermore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-cut$$\end{document} plots for fuzzy analysis are presented in Fig. 9a–c, depicting the importance of triangle fuzzy numbers in our methodology. Figure 9d,e both show the increasing behavior of the velocity components with rising values of the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} at lower and upper bounds respectively.

Furthermore, 3D plots have been generated in Fig. 10a,b representing solution along the fractional order versus time. In the same figure, parts (c) and (d) display the contour plots of the solution along \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p-cut$$\end{document} and time simultaneously.

Conclusion

This study focuses on solving and analyzing time-fractional and fuzzy-fractional (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ models of two distinct types. The research offers valuable insights that enable researchers to effectively tackle complex, highly nonlinear real-world problems that are both fractional and fuzzy-fractional in nature. To achieve this, a highly efficient technique known as the He-Laplace-Carson method is employed. Approximate solutions and residual errors are presented through 3D graphs and detailed contour diagrams. The influence of the fractional parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} on the velocity component profile is illustrated graphically, using comprehensive two-dimensional plots, as well as in tabular form. The model’s convergence is validated through tabular results, demonstrating that as the number of iterations increases, the series solution converges to the analytical solution as the residual error keeps on decreasing. Additionally, the reliability of the method is confirmed by calculating the residual errors in detailed tabular form. The results are divided into two sections, first we consider problem 1 and present both its fractional as well as fuzzy-fractional analysis and in the second section, the same discussion is presented for problem 2, which is another singular form of the (3 + 1) dimensional Burgers’ model under discussion. In the fractional analysis of both problems, it was observed that the velocity component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} and its associated components increase with progressing time and increasing x-values. Conversely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi (t,x,y,z)$$\end{document} appears to decrease over time with increasing y and z spatial coordinates. The fuzzy analysis corroborates these trends but provides a more detailed understanding by examining the behavior at both lower and upper bounds, in addition to the central point considered in traditional fractional analysis. Moreover, the fuzzy analysis reveals that as the fractional order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta$$\end{document} increases, the velocity component of the coupled Burgers’ model exhibits a consistent rise at both its lower and upper bounds for both problems investigated. Additionally, time shows a direct relation to the velocity component as well for all problems under consideration. This combined approach offers a more comprehensive understanding of the model’s behavior under both non-local effects and inherent uncertainties. The He-Laplace-Carson algorithm proves to be an effective approach for solving the specified (3 + 1) dimensional coupled Burgers’ models with minimal errors and enhanced series solutions. In future, proposed algorithm can be further enhance in several ways. First, it can be applied to other nonlinear fractional and fuzzy-fractional differential systems that appear in areas like fluid mechanics, control systems, and biological modeling. Second, the method can be improved by combining it with other numerical techniques to make it even more accurate and faster. Third, future work could focus on including both random and fuzzy uncertainties together to make the models more realistic. Finally, testing the method with real experimental data, for example from traffic flow, turbulence, or population studies, will help to show its practical usefulness.
